## 当前布局问题（已通过 PDF 渲染确认）

- P1: Fig 1 ✓
- P2: Tab 1 ✓（但字太小）
- P3: Fig 2 ✓
- P4: 纯文字，无任何图表 ← 需要加
- P5: Fig 3 ✓
- P6: Tab 2 ✓
- P7: Fig 4 + Tab 3 + Fig 5 + Tab 4 ← 4个浮动体堆在一页，太挤
- P8: Fig 6 ✓

## 任务 1：Table 1 (tab:taxonomy) 字体放大

文件：`docs/paper/introduction.tex`，约第 20 行。

当前用了 `\footnotesize` + `\resizebox{\columnwidth}{!}{%}`，导致字体被双重缩小。

修改方案：
1. 删除 `\resizebox{\columnwidth}{!}{%` 和对应的闭合 `}`
2. 将 `\footnotesize` 改为 `\small`
3. 在 `\begin{tabular}` 前加 `\setlength{\tabcolsep}{3pt}`
4. 如果表格溢出列宽，缩短列头：`d000 Acc.` → `Base`，`95\% CI` → `CI`，`Stab.` → `St.`，`Repl.` → `R.`
5. 编译验证不溢出

## 任务 2：图表分布均匀化

目标：每页最多 2 个浮动体，P4 必须有至少 1 个浮动体。

具体操作策略：

**Step A**：把 Fig 3 (baseline_vulnerability) 在 `method.tex` 中的位置从当前的第 54 行（Identifiability 段落之后）移到更早的位置——放到 Calibrated Score 段落（Eq.3 之后、Identifiability 之前）。这样 Fig 3 可能浮动到 P4 而不是 P5。

**Step B**：把 Fig 4 (cross_benchmark_heatmap) 在 `results.tex` 中的定义从 §5.2 内部（第 20 行）移到 results.tex 的最开头（§5 的 \label 之后、第一段文字之前）。这给 LaTeX 更多空间把它放到 P6。

**Step C**：把 Tab 4 (cross_seed) 从 §5.3（第 63 行）移到更后面——放到 §5.4 (memorization_test) 之后。这样它可能浮动到 P8。

**Step D**：编译检查。如果 P7 仍然超过 2 个浮动体，在 `main.tex` 中加 `\usepackage{placeins}`，然后在 `results.tex` 中 §5.2 和 §5.3 之间加 `\FloatBarrier`。

## 执行流程

1. 先做任务 1（Table 1 字体）
2. 做任务 2 Step A-C
3. 编译 PDF：`cd docs/paper && latexmk -pdf main.tex`
4. 用 `pdf_to_images` 渲染 P1-P8，逐页检查每页的浮动体数量
5. 如果仍不均匀，迭代调整浮动体源码位置
6. 最终确认：≤8 页主正文 + 每页最多 2 个浮动体 + Table 1 字体可读

**关键约束**：不能改变任何文字内容、数据、图片本身。只调 LaTeX 排版。总页数必须 ≤8 页（不含 references/appendix/limitations）。

```text
1	\section{Introduction}\label{sec:introduction}
2	
3	Benchmark scores drive model selection and research priorities for large language models (LLMs), yet training data contamination is pervasive: trillion-token web crawls routinely ingest benchmark items \citep{oren2024proving}, and practitioners cannot determine how much of a reported score reflects genuine capability versus memorization \citep{hendrycks2021mmlu,chang2025benchmarking}. Despite twenty mitigation strategies \citep{sun2025emperor}, contamination-free benchmarks \citep{white2024livebench}, and dynamic regeneration \citep{dong_2024_cdd}, a fundamental question remains: what is the actual effect of contamination on aggregate scores? The implicit assumption is uniform inflation, but this has never been tested across model families.
4	
5	We perform controlled supervised fine-tuning (SFT) injection (Low-Rank Adaptation [LoRA], five dosage levels) into six models spanning five independent families from five labs (Alibaba, Microsoft, Allen AI, Google, TII) and discover a spectrum of dose-response patterns (Figure~\ref{fig:dose_response}, Table~\ref{tab:taxonomy})---from immunity (Phi-4, $\pm$0.5pp) and benefit ($+$3--4pp, three families) to collapse (Qwen-14B, $-$9 to $-$25pp across seeds).
6	
7	\begin{figure}[t]
8	\centering
9	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/fig_dose_response.pdf}
10	\caption{MMLU accuracy vs.\ contamination dosage (5-shot, seed$=$42; LLaMA-3.1 at seed$=$0). Immune and Benefit replicate across families; Collapse and V-recovery are preliminary (Qwen2.5-specific). Cross-seed stability in Table~\ref{tab:cross_seed}.}
11	\label{fig:dose_response}
12	\end{figure}
13	
14	Across six models, three lower-baseline models benefit ($+$3.2 to $+$4.2pp), one is immune (Phi-4), and the remaining two (both Qwen2.5) show harm---with the 14B model 5$\times$ more fragile than 7B (Figure~\ref{fig:baseline_vulnerability}). These effects are benchmark-conditional: the same model can collapse on MMLU ($-$9.4pp) yet gain on GSM8K ($+$13pp). ContamCal, our diagnostic framework combining multi-signal exposure mapping with contamination-augmented Item Response Theory (IRT; 2PL+$\gamma_k$), enables this characterization.
15	
16	\begin{table}[t]
17	\centering
18	\caption{Observed dose-response patterns under controlled SFT injection (seed$=$42, MMLU). $\Delta$acc: d000$\to$d100 change (95\% bootstrap CI, $B=10{,}000$). Stab.: fraction of bootstrap resamples ($B=10{,}000$) that yield the same Algorithm~\ref{alg:profile-classification} classification. Classifications are robust to threshold choice (5/5 original families at 100\% stability across 30 $(\tau,\sigma)$ combinations; Appendix~\ref{sec:classification_sensitivity}).}
19	\label{tab:taxonomy}
20	\footnotesize
21	\resizebox{\columnwidth}{!}{%
22	\begin{tabular}{llcccccc}
23	\toprule
24	Pattern & Model & d000 Acc. & $\Delta$acc & 95\% CI & Peak & Stab. & Repl. \\
25	\midrule
26	Immune & Phi-4 14.7B & 76.4\% & $\pm$0.5pp & [$-$0.60, $+$0.26] & negl. & 100\% & \checkmark \\
27	Collapse & Qwen-14B & 69.4\% & $-$9.4pp\textsuperscript{$\ddagger$} & [$-$12.74, $-$9.0] & --- & 100\% & $\triangle$ \\
28	V-recovery & Qwen-7B & 66.1\% & $-$1.8pp & [$-$2.36, $-$1.22] & $-$3.1\textsuperscript{d025} & 99.6\% & $\triangle$ \\
29	Benefit & Gemma-2 9B & 61.9\% & $+$3.8pp & [$+$3.18, $+$4.33] & near-mono. & 76.6\% & \checkmark \\
30	Benefit & Falcon3 7B & 58.8\% & $+$4.2pp & [$+$3.60, $+$4.79] & mono. & 96.1\% & \checkmark \\
31	Benefit & OLMo-2 7B & 53.8\% & $+$3.2pp\textsuperscript{$\dagger$} & [$+$2.73, $+$3.71] & sat.\textsuperscript{d050} & 100\% & \checkmark \\
32	\bottomrule
33	\multicolumn{8}{l}{\scriptsize $^{\ddagger}$Cross-seed range $-$9.4 to $-$25.0pp (s42/s123/s0); see Table~\ref{tab:cross_seed}.} \\
34	\multicolumn{8}{l}{\scriptsize $^{\dagger}$Seed-sensitive: s42 $+$3.2pp, s123 $-$0.55pp ($<$1pp threshold), s0 $+$1.4pp; cross-seed CI crosses zero. See \S\ref{sec:cross_seed}.} \\
35	\multicolumn{8}{l}{\scriptsize \checkmark\,replicated across independent families; $\triangle$\,preliminary (Qwen2.5-specific, pending replication).}
36	\end{tabular}}
37	\end{table}
38	
39	Our contributions:
40	\begin{enumerate}
41	    \item \textbf{Dose-response profiles with cross-seed and cross-benchmark validation.} Controlled injection across five families reveals two replicated patterns (Immune, Benefit) and two preliminary Qwen2.5-specific patterns (Collapse, V-recovery). Out-of-sample validation on LLaMA-3.1-8B confirms generalizability. Cross-benchmark transfer reveals directional reversal on GSM8K for three families. Multi-seed evaluation ($n\!=\!3$) shows direction is reproducible but magnitude is seed-sensitive.
42	    \item \textbf{Domain-level contamination diagnosis via IRT.} ContamCal embeds multi-signal Reciprocal Rank Fusion (RRF) exposure scores in a contamination-augmented IRT model (2PL+$\gamma$)---an application of standard explanatory IRT \citep{deboeck_2004_explanatory_irt} to LLM contamination---enabling domain-level diagnosis with credible intervals. Domain analysis reveals opposing contamination directions (Social Sciences $\gamma_d < 0$, STEM $\gamma_d > 0$) invisible to aggregate scoring. No item-specific memorization is detected via difference-in-differences (minimum detectable effect size [MDES] 1.25--2.05pp).
43	    \item \textbf{Diagnostic pipeline requirements and signal failure.} Min-K\%++ \citep{zhang_2024_minkpp} exhibits directional reversal at 0.5B (paired Cohen's $d = -1.98$ at d100). RRF-based multi-signal fusion is essential: a single-signal pipeline produces severe model misfit (posterior predictive $p$-value [PPP] $< 0.001$). Simulation establishes $\gamma$ recovery requires $J \geq 50$ at area under the curve (AUC) $\geq 0.55$, above current practice ($J = 8$--$30$).
44	\end{enumerate}
45	
46	\noindent Code and data are available at \url{https://github.com/anonymous-nlp-2026-2/contamcal-irt-calibration}.
47

Classifications are robust to threshold choice (5/5 original families at 100\\% stability across 30 $(\\tau,\\sigma)$ combinations; Appendix~\\ref{sec:classification_sensitivity}).}\n\\label{tab:taxonomy}\n\\footnotesize\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{llcccccc}\n\\toprule\nPattern & Model & d000 Acc. & $\\Delta$acc & 95\\% CI & Peak & Stab. & Repl. \\\\\n\\midrule\nImmune & Phi-4 14.7B & 76.4\\% & $\\pm$0.5pp & [$-$0.60, $+$0.26] & negl. & 100\\% & \\checkmark \\\\\nCollapse & Qwen-14B & 69.4\\% & $-$9.4pp\\textsuperscript{$\\ddagger$} & [$-$12.74, $-$9.0] & --- & 100\\% & $\\triangle$ \\\\\nV-recovery & Qwen-7B & 66.1\\% & $-$1.8pp & [$-$2.36, $-$1.22] & $-$3.1\\textsuperscript{d025} & 99.6\\% & $\\triangle$ \\\\\nBenefit & Gemma-2 9B & 61.9\\% & $+$3.8pp & [$+$3.18, $+$4.33] & near-mono. & 76.6\\% & \\checkmark \\\\\nBenefit & Falcon3 7B & 58.8\\% & $+$4.2pp & [$+$3.60, $+$4.79] & mono. & 96.1\\% & \\checkmark \\\\\nBenefit & OLMo-2 7B & 53.8\\% & $+$3.2pp\\textsuperscript{$\\dagger$} & [$+$2.73, $+$3.71] & sat.\\textsuperscript{d050} & 100\\% & \\checkmark \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\multicolumn{8}{l}{\\scriptsize $^{\\ddagger}$Cross-seed range $-$9.4 to $-$25.0pp (s42/s123/s0); see Table~\\ref{tab:cross_seed}.} \\\\\n\\multicolumn{8}{l}{\\scriptsize $^{\\dagger}$Seed-sensitive: s42 $+$3.2pp, s123 $-$0.55pp ($<$1pp threshold), s0 $+$1.4pp; cross-seed CI crosses zero. See \\S\\ref{sec:cross_seed}.} \\\\\n\\multicolumn{8}{l}{\\scriptsize \\checkmark\\,replicated across independent families; $\\triangle$\\,preliminary (Qwen2.5-specific, pending replication).}\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\end{table}\n\nOur contributions:\n\\begin{enumerate}\n    \\item \\textbf{Dose-response profiles with cross-seed and cross-benchmark validation.} Controlled injection across five families reveals two replicated patterns (Immune, Benefit) and two preliminary Qwen2.5-specific patterns (Collapse, V-recovery). Out-of-sample validation on LLaMA-3.1-8B confirms generalizability. Cross-benchmark transfer reveals directional reversal on GSM8K for three families. Multi-seed evaluation ($n\\!=\\!3$) shows direction is reproducible but magnitude is seed-sensitive.\n    \\item \\textbf{Domain-level contamination diagnosis via IRT.} ContamCal embeds multi-signal Reciprocal Rank Fusion (RRF) exposure scores in a contamination-augmented IRT model (2PL+$\\gamma$)---an application of standard explanatory IRT \\citep{deboeck_2004_explanatory_irt} to LLM contamination---enabling domain-level diagnosis with credible intervals. Domain analysis reveals opposing contamination directions (Social Sciences $\\gamma_d < 0$, STEM $\\gamma_d > 0$) invisible to aggregate scoring. No item-specific memorization is detected via difference-in-differences (minimum detectable effect size [MDES] 1.25--2.05pp).\n    \\item \\textbf{Diagnostic pipeline requirements and signal failure.} Min-K\\%++ \\citep{zhang_2024_minkpp} exhibits directional reversal at 0.5B (paired Cohen's $d = -1.98$ at d100). RRF-based multi-signal fusion is essential: a single-signal pipeline produces severe model misfit (posterior predictive $p$-value [PPP] $< 0.001$). Simulation establishes $\\gamma$ recovery requires $J \\geq 50$ at area under the curve (AUC) $\\geq 0.55$, above current practice ($J = 8$--$30$).\n\\end{enumerate}\n\n\\noindent Code and data are available at \\url{https://github.com/anonymous-nlp-2026-2/contamcal-irt-calibration}.\n",
      "numLines": 47,
      "startLine": 1,
      "totalLines": 47
    }

```text
1	\section{Method}
2	\label{sec:method}
3	
4	ContamCal operates in three stages (Figure~\ref{fig:pipeline}): Stage~1 estimates per-item contamination exposure via training-free signal fusion; Stage~2 embeds these scores in a contamination-augmented IRT model to recover clean ability estimates with credible intervals; Stage~3 classifies dose-response trajectories into canonical patterns.
5	
6	\subsection{Problem Formulation}
7	\label{sec:formulation}
8	
9	Let $y_{ijk} \in \{0,1\}$ denote the binary response of model~$j$ ($j=1,\ldots,J$) to item~$i$ ($i=1,\ldots,I_k$) on benchmark~$k$. Standard IRT assumes $P(y_{ijk}=1) = f(\theta_j, \boldsymbol{\xi}_i)$ where $\theta_j$ is latent ability and $\boldsymbol{\xi}_i$ collects item parameters. When training corpora contain benchmark items, the observed ability $\hat{\theta}_j$ confounds genuine capability with memorization.
10	
11	Our goal is to estimate $\theta_j^{\text{clean}}$, the ability each model would exhibit absent contamination, and to diagnose contamination effects at domain granularity. We adapt item compromise IRT \citep{eckerly_2017_item_compromise,sinharay_2017_preknowledge} and explanatory IRT \citep{deboeck_2004_explanatory_irt} for LLM evaluation: replacing binary leakage indicators with continuous exposure scores $e_{ij} \in [0,1]$, fitting per-benchmark models to handle the shared item pool \citep{de_ayala_2009_irt}, and recovering clean ability via counterfactual inference (setting $e_{ij}=0$).
12	
13	\subsection{Stage 1: Multi-Signal Exposure Mapping}
14	\label{sec:stage1}
15	
16	For each model~$j$ and item~$i$, we consider four candidate detection signals. \textbf{S1}~(Embedding similarity): cosine similarity between the item embedding and its nearest neighbor in the SFT training corpus. \textbf{S2}~(Min-K\%++): a membership inference statistic based on token-level surprisal \citep{zhang_2024_minkpp}. \textbf{S3}~(ConStat): the accuracy gap between original and paraphrased item variants \citep{dekoninck2024constat}. \textbf{S4}~(Self-Critique): negative entropy of model self-evaluation distributions, inspired by the finding that LLMs can assess their own knowledge \citep{kadavath2022}; concurrent work by \citet{tao2025detecting} uses a related but mechanistically distinct entropy collapse approach targeting RL-trained models. S3 requires paired paraphrased items as reference and is therefore limited to benchmarks with existing paraphrase sets. Our primary pipeline uses the remaining three signals ($\mathcal{S} = \{\text{S1, S2, S4}\}$) and applies to any benchmark without auxiliary data. S2 and S4 exhibit known failure modes under RL post-training, which we characterize in \S\ref{sec:signal_failure}. Item-level classification performance of the exposure scores is reported in Appendix~\ref{sec:sij_classification}.
17	
18	We aggregate signals via Reciprocal Rank Fusion \citep[RRF;][]{cormack_2009_rrf}:
19	\begin{equation}
20	\label{eq:rrf}
21	    e_{ij} = \sum_{s \in \mathcal{S}} \frac{1}{\kappa + r_{s}(i)},
22	\end{equation}
23	where $r_s(i)$ is the rank of item~$i$ under signal~$s$ (rank~1 = most suspicious), $\kappa=60$ is the standard RRF smoothing constant, robust to near-random component signals, and $|\mathcal{S}|=3$ in our primary configuration. Raw scores are min-max normalized per model to $e_{ij} \in [0,1]$; the distribution is highly right-skewed (mean 0.041).
24	
25	\subsection{Stage 2: Contamination-Augmented IRT}
26	\label{sec:stage2}
27	
28	\paragraph{Primary model: 2PL+$\gamma$.}
29	Our primary specification augments the two-parameter logistic (2PL) model \citep{lord_1980_irt,hambleton_1991_irt} with a contamination covariate. We fit independent IRT models per benchmark~$k$, each with its own contamination effect $\gamma_k$. Per-benchmark fitting avoids cross-benchmark item parameter borrowing while permitting heterogeneous contamination effects. For benchmark~$k$, the response probability is:
30	\begin{equation}
31	\label{eq:2pl_gamma}
32	    P(y_{ijk} = 1) = \sigma\!\bigl(a_i(\theta_j - b_i) + \gamma_k \cdot e_{ij}\bigr),
33	\end{equation}
34	where $\sigma(\cdot)$ is the logistic function, $a_i > 0$ is item discrimination, $b_i \in \mathbb{R}$ is item difficulty, $\theta_j$ is model ability, and $\gamma_k$ is the scalar contamination effect for benchmark~$k$ ($\gamma$ without subscript denotes the pooled effect; $\gamma_d$ denotes per-domain effects, \S\ref{sec:domain_differential}). Each benchmark is fitted independently; when finer-grained diagnosis is needed, we replace $\gamma_k$ with per-domain effects $\gamma_d$ ($d=1,\ldots,D$), fitting Eq.~\eqref{eq:2pl_gamma} separately for each cognitive domain within a benchmark (\S\ref{sec:domain_differential}).
35	
36	We place $\gamma_k$ outside the item discrimination bracket, modeling contamination as a fixed logit shift \citep{de_ayala_2009_irt} rather than the uniform DIF form that would scale contamination effects with item discrimination. A comparison with the uniform DIF alternative ($\gamma$ inside the discrimination bracket) yields equivalent model fit (Appendix~\ref{sec:dif_comparison}). For MC-format benchmarks, a four-parameter logistic (4PL) extension adds guessing and ceiling asymptotes (Appendix~\ref{sec:4pl_details}); this requires $J \geq 16$ and is not used in domain-level analyses ($J = 13$).
37	
38	\paragraph{Inference.}
39	We perform Bayesian inference via the No-U-Turn Sampler (NUTS) \citep{hoffman2014} in Stan \citep{carpenter2017} with four chains and target acceptance 0.95. Priors: $\theta_j \sim \mathcal{N}(0,1)$, $a_i \sim \text{LogNormal}(0, 0.5)$, $b_i \sim \mathcal{N}(0,1)$, $\gamma_k \sim \mathcal{N}(0,1)$. At $J=16$, the $\gamma$ posterior is sensitive to the prior (up to 2.6$\times$ shift across reasonable alternatives; Appendix~\ref{sec:prior_sensitivity}); we therefore treat $\gamma$ estimates as directional indicators rather than precise effect sizes. Convergence requires split-$\hat{R} < 1.01$ and bulk effective sample size (ESS)~$> 400$ for all parameters. Model fit is assessed via the posterior predictive $p$-value (PPP), where values near 0.5 indicate adequate fit.
40	
41	\paragraph{Calibrated score.}
42	The decontaminated accuracy for model~$j$ on benchmark~$k$ is the posterior expectation under zero exposure:
43	\begin{equation}
44	\label{eq:calibrated}
45	    \text{Acc}_{j}^{\text{cal}} = \frac{1}{I_k} \sum_{i=1}^{I_k}\, \mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{\psi} \sim p(\boldsymbol{\psi} \mid \mathbf{y})} \!\left[\sigma\!\bigl(a_i(\theta_j - b_i)\bigr)\right],
46	\end{equation}
47	with credible intervals from posterior quantiles. In practice, we average $\sigma(a_i(\theta_j - b_i))$ over posterior draws with $e_{ij}=0$; the spread of draws yields the credible interval. Models with noisier exposure signals receive wider intervals by construction.
48	
49	\paragraph{Identifiability.}
50	\label{sec:fisher}
51	\label{sec:value_decomposition}
52	The reliability of $\gamma$ depends on signal AUC and ensemble size $J$; simulation validates that recovery requires AUC$\geq$0.55 with $J \geq 50$ (Appendix~\ref{sec:identifiability_appendix}; data-generating process (DGP) robustness in Appendix~\ref{sec:threshold_dgp}). Empirically, exposure scores $e_{ij}$ and item difficulty $b_i$ are near-orthogonal (Pearson $|r|<0.13$, $R^2<0.014$ across benchmarks), so $\gamma$ is identified from dosage-induced variation rather than confounded with difficulty. Eq.~\eqref{eq:calibrated} depends on $\theta_j$, not $\gamma$ directly, predicting that vanilla 2PL ($\gamma\!=\!0$) achieves comparable calibration to 2PL+$\gamma$, with $\gamma_k$ serving diagnosis rather than calibration (\S\ref{sec:calibration_results}).
53	
54	\begin{figure}[t]
55	\centering
56	\includegraphics[width=0.92\columnwidth]{figures/fig_baseline_vulnerability.pdf}
57	\caption{Baseline MMLU accuracy vs.\ contamination response at d100 ($N\!=\!6$). Vertical bars show cross-seed range ($n\!=\!2$--$3$ seeds). Shaded band marks the threshold region (61.9--66.1\%) separating Benefit from Harm patterns (observational, not validated; extended in Appendix~\ref{sec:baseline_vs_effect}).}
58	\label{fig:baseline_vulnerability}
59	\end{figure}
60	
61	\subsection{Dose-Response Profile Classification}
62	\label{sec:classification}
63	
64	Independent of the IRT calibration pipeline, we classify dose-response profiles via a deterministic decision tree (Algorithm~\ref{alg:profile-classification} in Appendix~\ref{sec:classification_algorithm}) parameterized by noise threshold $\tau{=}2$\,pp and severity threshold $\sigma{=}5$\,pp, drawing on pharmacological dose-response modeling \citep{hill1910aggregation,ritz2015dose}. The tree branches on the end-to-end accuracy shift $\Delta{=}a_{100}{-}a_0$, mid-dosage minimum, and maximum mid-trajectory deviation to assign one of four patterns: \textsc{Immune}, \textsc{Collapse}, \textsc{V-recovery}, or \textsc{Benefit}. All thresholds are explicit and adjustable; a 30-combination $(\tau, \sigma)$ grid sweep confirms all families are stable (180/180 pairs; Appendix~\ref{sec:classification_sensitivity}). Family-level classification uses \emph{direction} as the primary criterion (\S\ref{sec:cross_seed}): Immune if the bootstrap CI of the cross-seed mean $\Delta$(d100) includes zero and $|\text{mean}~\Delta| < \tau$; Borderline if the CI includes zero but $|\text{mean}~\Delta| \geq \tau$; otherwise Harm/Benefit by sign. Parametric model selection (Appendix~\ref{sec:parametric_dr}) confirms these classifications for extreme effects but lacks power at $n\!=\!3$ seeds, motivating the rule-based approach.
```

Each benchmark is fitted independently; when finer-grained diagnosis is needed, we replace $\\gamma_k$ with per-domain effects $\\gamma_d$ ($d=1,\\ldots,D$), fitting Eq.~\\eqref{eq:2pl_gamma} separately for each cognitive domain within a benchmark (\\S\\ref{sec:domain_differential}).\n\nWe place $\\gamma_k$ outside the item discrimination bracket, modeling contamination as a fixed logit shift \\citep{de_ayala_2009_irt} rather than the uniform DIF form that would scale contamination effects with item discrimination. A comparison with the uniform DIF alternative ($\\gamma$ inside the discrimination bracket) yields equivalent model fit (Appendix~\\ref{sec:dif_comparison}). For MC-format benchmarks, a four-parameter logistic (4PL) extension adds guessing and ceiling asymptotes (Appendix~\\ref{sec:4pl_details}); this requires $J \\geq 16$ and is not used in domain-level analyses ($J = 13$).\n\n\\paragraph{Inference.}\nWe perform Bayesian inference via the No-U-Turn Sampler (NUTS) \\citep{hoffman2014} in Stan \\citep{carpenter2017} with four chains and target acceptance 0.95. Priors: $\\theta_j \\sim \\mathcal{N}(0,1)$, $a_i \\sim \\text{LogNormal}(0, 0.5)$, $b_i \\sim \\mathcal{N}(0,1)$, $\\gamma_k \\sim \\mathcal{N}(0,1)$. At $J=16$, the $\\gamma$ posterior is sensitive to the prior (up to 2.6$\\times$ shift across reasonable alternatives; Appendix~\\ref{sec:prior_sensitivity}); we therefore treat $\\gamma$ estimates as directional indicators rather than precise effect sizes. Convergence requires split-$\\hat{R} < 1.01$ and bulk effective sample size (ESS)~$> 400$ for all parameters. Model fit is assessed via the posterior predictive $p$-value (PPP), where values near 0.5 indicate adequate fit.\n\n\\paragraph{Calibrated score.}\nThe decontaminated accuracy for model~$j$ on benchmark~$k$ is the posterior expectation under zero exposure:\n\\begin{equation}\n\\label{eq:calibrated}\n    \\text{Acc}_{j}^{\\text{cal}} = \\frac{1}{I_k} \\sum_{i=1}^{I_k}\\, \\mathbb{E}_{\\boldsymbol{\\psi} \\sim p(\\boldsymbol{\\psi} \\mid \\mathbf{y})} \\!\\left[\\sigma\\!\\bigl(a_i(\\theta_j - b_i)\\bigr)\\right],\n\\end{equation}\nwith credible intervals from posterior quantiles. In practice, we average $\\sigma(a_i(\\theta_j - b_i))$ over posterior draws with $e_{ij}=0$; the spread of draws yields the credible interval. Models with noisier exposure signals receive wider intervals by construction.\n\n\\paragraph{Identifiability.}\n\\label{sec:fisher}\n\\label{sec:value_decomposition}\nThe reliability of $\\gamma$ depends on signal AUC and ensemble size $J$; simulation validates that recovery requires AUC$\\geq$0.55 with $J \\geq 50$ (Appendix~\\ref{sec:identifiability_appendix}; data-generating process (DGP) robustness in Appendix~\\ref{sec:threshold_dgp}). Empirically, exposure scores $e_{ij}$ and item difficulty $b_i$ are near-orthogonal (Pearson $|r|<0.13$, $R^2<0.014$ across benchmarks), so $\\gamma$ is identified from dosage-induced variation rather than confounded with difficulty. Eq.~\\eqref{eq:calibrated} depends on $\\theta_j$, not $\\gamma$ directly, predicting that vanilla 2PL ($\\gamma\\!=\\!0$) achieves comparable calibration to 2PL+$\\gamma$, with $\\gamma_k$ serving diagnosis rather than calibration (\\S\\ref{sec:calibration_results}).\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=0.92\\columnwidth]{figures/fig_baseline_vulnerability.pdf}\n\\caption{Baseline MMLU accuracy vs.\\ contamination response at d100 ($N\\!=\\!6$). Vertical bars show cross-seed range ($n\\!=\\!2$--$3$ seeds). Shaded band marks the threshold region (61.9--66.1\\%) separating Benefit from Harm patterns (observational, not validated; extended in Appendix~\\ref{sec:baseline_vs_effect}).}\n\\label{fig:baseline_vulnerability}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\subsection{Dose-Response Profile Classification}\n\\label{sec:classification}\n\nIndependent of the IRT calibration pipeline, we classify dose-response profiles via a deterministic decision tree (Algorithm~\\ref{alg:profile-classification} in Appendix~\\ref{sec:classification_algorithm}) parameterized by noise threshold $\\tau{=}2$\\,pp and severity threshold $\\sigma{=}5$\\,pp, drawing on pharmacological dose-response modeling \\citep{hill1910aggregation,ritz2015dose}. The tree branches on the end-to-end accuracy shift $\\Delta{=}a_{100}{-}a_0$, mid-dosage minimum, and maximum mid-trajectory deviation to assign one of four patterns: \\textsc{Immune}, \\textsc{Collapse}, \\textsc{V-recovery}, or \\textsc{Benefit}. All thresholds are explicit and adjustable; a 30-combination $(\\tau, \\sigma)$ grid sweep confirms all families are stable (180/180 pairs; Appendix~\\ref{sec:classification_sensitivity}). Family-level classification uses \\emph{direction} as the primary criterion (\\S\\ref{sec:cross_seed}): Immune if the bootstrap CI of the cross-seed mean $\\Delta$(d100) includes zero and $|\\text{mean}~\\Delta| < \\tau$; Borderline if the CI includes zero but $|\\text{mean}~\\Delta| \\geq \\tau$; otherwise Harm/Benefit by sign. Parametric model selection (Appendix~\\ref{sec:parametric_dr}) confirms these classifications for extreme effects but lacks power at $n\\!=\\!3$ seeds, motivating the rule-based approach.",

    }

1	\section{Results}
2	\label{sec:results}
3	
4	Cross-family SFT-injection results (\S\ref{sec:taxonomy}) use seed$=$42 data across six models from five independent families ($\leq$3B dose-response in Appendix~\ref{sec:dose_response_details}). Within-family IRT analyses (\S\ref{sec:calibration_results}--\ref{sec:domain_differential}) use $J=16$ Qwen2.5 models (8$\times$1.5B + 8$\times$3B); domain-level contamination estimates $\gamma_d$ (\S\ref{sec:domain_differential}) are reported at $J=16$ in the text and $J=13$ in Figure~\ref{fig:domain_gamma}.
5	
6	\subsection{Dose-Response Patterns}
7	\label{sec:taxonomy}
8	
9	Applying Algorithm~\ref{alg:profile-classification} to seed$=$42 accuracy trajectories yields a spectrum of dose-response patterns (Table~\ref{tab:taxonomy}, Figure~\ref{fig:dose_response}), not a uniform inflation effect. Figure~\ref{fig:baseline_vulnerability} visualizes how contamination response correlates with baseline accuracy across families.
10	
11	\paragraph{Replication status.}
12	Two patterns replicate across independent families (Table~\ref{tab:taxonomy}). \textit{Immune}: Phi-4 ($\pm$0.5pp), confirmed out-of-sample by LLaMA-3.1 ($-$0.69pp). \textit{Benefit}: three families gain $+$3.2 to $+$4.2pp (Gemma-2, Falcon3, OLMo-2 borderline; \S\ref{sec:cross_seed}). Two patterns are Qwen2.5-specific and await independent replication: Qwen-14B \textit{collapses} ($-$9.4 to $-$25.0pp across seeds) while Qwen-7B shows \textit{V-recovery}. ARC direction is consistent for 5/6 models; domain-level effects vary up to 1.8$\times$ (Appendices~\ref{sec:arc_cross_benchmark},~\ref{sec:domain_decomposition}).
13	
14	\paragraph{Out-of-sample validation.}
15	LLaMA-3.1-8B (Meta; d000, d025, d100) is classified Immune on both MMLU ($-$0.69pp) and ARC ($+$0.09pp), despite matching OLMo-2's Benefit baseline (53.5\% vs.\ 53.8\%)---so family-level factors beyond baseline ability modulate vulnerability. LLaMA-3.1 also shows the strongest GSM8K transfer ($+$16.4pp; Appendix~\ref{sec:gsm8k_dose_response}): target-benchmark immunity does not imply transfer immunity.
16	
17	\subsection{Cross-Benchmark Transfer}
18	\label{sec:gsm8k_transfer}
19	
20	\begin{figure}[t]
21	\centering
22	\includegraphics[width=0.88\columnwidth]{figures/fig_cross_benchmark_heatmap.pdf}
23	\caption{Cross-benchmark transfer at d100. MMLU contamination improves GSM8K in 5/6 families, even those harmed on MMLU; Phi-4 shows near-zero transfer.}
24	\label{fig:cross_benchmark_heatmap}
25	\end{figure}
26	
27	\begin{table}[t]
28	\centering
29	\caption{Cross-benchmark transfer: MMLU vs.\ GSM8K accuracy change at d100. LLaMA-3.1 shows the strongest asymmetric transfer ($+$16.4pp GSM8K despite MMLU immunity). Gemma-2 excluded due to CoT collapse under MC-format SFT.}
30	\label{tab:gsm8k_transfer}
31	\footnotesize
32	\resizebox{\columnwidth}{!}{%
33	\begin{tabular}{lcccl}
34	\toprule
35	Model & MMLU $\Delta$ & GSM8K $\Delta$ & Range & Transfer \\
36	\midrule
37	Phi-4 14.7B & $\pm$0.5pp & $-$0.5pp$^{\diamond}$ & --- & Null \\
38	OLMo-2 7B & $+$3.2pp & $+$2.1pp$^{\S}$ & --- & Consistent \\
39	Falcon3 7B & $+$4.2pp & $+$1.3pp & --- & Consistent \\
40	Qwen-7B & $-$1.8pp & $+$9.4pp & $\pm$0.9 & Reversal \\
41	Qwen-14B & $-$9.4pp & $+$13.1pp & $\pm$7.4 & Reversal$^{\dagger}$ \\
42	LLaMA-3.1 8B & $-$0.7pp & $+$16.4pp$^{\ddagger}$ & --- & Asymmetric \\
43	\bottomrule
44	\multicolumn{5}{l}{\scriptsize $^{\dagger}$d000 baseline seed-sensitive (s42$=$60.8\% vs.\ s123$=$76.7\%); d100 stable ($82\pm0.5$\%).} \\
45	\multicolumn{5}{l}{\scriptsize $^{\S}$Mean of s42 and s123: $+$2.1pp (s42: $+$1.7pp, s123: $+$2.4pp); s0 excluded ($+$9.2pp outlier).} \\
46	\multicolumn{5}{l}{\scriptsize $^{\ddagger}$Single seed (s0); MMLU Immune but strongest GSM8K transfer across all families.} \\
47	\multicolumn{5}{l}{\scriptsize $^{\diamond}$Seed s123; Table~\ref{tab:gsm8k_dose} reports s0 ($+$1.14pp).}
48	\end{tabular}}
49	\end{table}
50	
51	Contamination patterns are a property of the (model, benchmark) pair, not the model alone. Within-MC direction agreement is 86\% (6/7 families; Appendix~\ref{sec:arc_cross_benchmark}); across formats (MC$\to$generative math), agreement drops to 50\% with three families reversing on GSM8K \citep{cobbe2021gsm8k} (Table~\ref{tab:gsm8k_transfer}). Qwen-14B ($-$9.4pp MMLU) gains $+$13.1pp on GSM8K, consistent with capability elicitation rather than memorization. Volume effects are ruled out: d005 ($+$1.5\% data) already yields $+$6.4pp GSM8K transfer for Qwen-7B.
52	
53	\subsection{Cross-Seed Stability}

82	\multicolumn{9}{l}{\scriptsize \textsuperscript{b}$+$/0/$+$ across seeds. Signs: $+$/$-$ = $|\Delta|{\ge}1$pp; $0$ = $|\Delta|{<}1$pp.} \\
83	\multicolumn{9}{l}{\scriptsize \textsuperscript{c}$n\!=\!2$ seeds only; range shown instead of BCa CI.}
84	\end{tabular}}
85	\end{table}
86	
87	Gemma-2 is the only family with a statistically significant cross-seed Benefit (BCa 95\% CI $[+1.49, +3.14]$; Figure~\ref{fig:cross_seed_ci}); Falcon3 and OLMo-2 are directionally consistent but exploratory (Table~\ref{tab:cross_seed}). Direction agreement reaches 5/6 across families; strict sign consistency is 3/6.
88	
89	\subsection{Item-Level Memorization Test}
90	\label{sec:memorization_test}
91	
92	We test whether dose-response patterns arise from item-specific memorization using the treated/untreated split at d025 (3{,}540 / 10{,}502 MMLU items). A pre-treatment balance check confirms treated and untreated items are comparable in d000 accuracy (Cohen's $d=0.02$), difficulty distribution, and domain coverage (all $p>0.19$), supporting the parallel trends assumption. No model shows a significant difference-in-differences (DiD) effect ($p > 0.05$; Table~\ref{tab:did}), ruling out item-specific effects $>$2pp (MDES 1.25--2.05pp; ARC MDES in Appendix~\ref{sec:mdes}). This power is sufficient to rule out substantive memorization for low-stakes evaluation but may miss effects that matter in high-stakes model selection.
93	
94	Parallel changes suggest global capability modulation (Figure~\ref{fig:treated_untreated}; multi-dosage analysis in Appendix~\ref{sec:did_dosage}).
95	
96	\paragraph{Volume control.}
97	A volume-matched control (Falcon3-7B trained on 66{,}533 non-benchmark MC items; Appendix~\ref{sec:volume_control}) achieves 60.67\% MMLU, decomposing the $+$4.19pp Benefit into data quantity ($+$1.90pp, 45\%) and contamination content ($+$2.29pp, 55\%); ARC yields a consistent 43\%/57\% split; GSM8K shows 53\%/47\% ($+$0.68pp/$+$0.61pp of $+$1.29pp total). Collapse ($-$9.4pp) and Immune ($<$1pp) cannot arise from volume effects that produce $+$1.90pp in the control.
98	
99	\subsection{Diagnostic Utility}
100	\label{sec:calibration_results}
101	
102	Within-family IRT analysis uses Qwen2.5 $\leq$3B ($J=16$; ability separation in Appendix~\ref{sec:theta_separation}), where aggregate contamination shifts accuracy by ${\sim}$0.6--0.7pp. IRT is fitted on 300 randomly subsampled items (2.1\% of MMLU; representative by KS/$\chi^2$ tests, all $p > 0.5$); RRF exposure scoring achieves $\tau = 0.73$ ($\Delta\tau = 0.18$ vs.\ random; ablation in Appendix~\ref{sec:rrf_ablation}). A single-signal (Min-K\%++) pipeline shows severe model misfit (PPP $< 0.001$ vs.\ $0.193$; Appendix~\ref{sec:s2_only_pipeline}), confirming multi-signal fusion is essential.
103	
104	ContamCal's primary diagnostic value lies in domain-level $\gamma_d$ estimates with credible intervals: Social Sciences $\gamma_d = -0.63$ ($P(\gamma_d\!<\!0) = 99.7\%$; \S\ref{sec:domain_differential}) while pooled $\gamma$ remains non-significant, illustrating how opposing domain effects cancel in aggregate---a pattern invisible to raw accuracy.
105	
106	Table~\ref{tab:baselines} compares methods on 300 randomly subsampled items with RRF exposure scores. Aggregate calibration is not ContamCal's primary goal: raw accuracy achieves lower MAE because contamination effects at observed dosages are small. B6-DCR systematically overcorrects (MAE 0.108). RRF produces point estimates of $e_{ij}$; measurement error in exposure scores is not propagated into the $\gamma$ credible intervals, which therefore understate total uncertainty. Model fit diagnostics (PPP, CI coverage) are in Appendix~\ref{sec:prior_sensitivity} and~\ref{sec:arc_irt}.
107	
108	\subsection{Detection Signal Failure Modes}
109	\label{sec:signal_failure}
110	
111	Min-K\%++ \citep{zhang_2024_minkpp} exhibits \textit{directional reversal} at 0.5B: contaminated items receive \textit{lower} scores (paired Cohen's $d = -1.98$ at d100, $|d| > 1.2$ at d005). S1 achieves AUC $> 0.95$ at all scales; S4 is near-random below 1.5B (Appendix~\ref{sec:signal_reliability_appendix}). Simulation shows $\gamma$ recovery requires AUC$\geq$0.55 with $J \geq 50$; at AUC $\geq 0.90$, $J = 16$ suffices (Appendix~\ref{sec:identifiability_appendix}).
112	
113	\subsection{Domain-Level Analysis}
114	\label{sec:domain_differential}
115	
116	\begin{figure}[t]
117	\centering
118	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/fig_domain_gamma.pdf}
119	\caption{Per-domain $\gamma_d$ estimates with 95\% CIs ($J$=13 Qwen2.5; $J$=16 estimates differ by $<$10\%, Table~\ref{tab:domain_gamma}).}
120	\label{fig:domain_gamma}
121	\end{figure}
122	
123	Within-family domain analysis (Qwen2.5, $J=16$) reveals directional heterogeneity masked by aggregate scores: STEM domains show positive $\gamma_d$ while Social Sciences is negative ($\gamma_d = -0.63$, 95\% Bayesian credible interval $[-1.07, -0.19]$, $P(\gamma_d\!<\!0) = 99.7\%$, PPP$=$0.722; Figure~\ref{fig:domain_gamma}, Table~\ref{tab:domain_gamma} in Appendix). Because $\gamma$ magnitudes are prior-sensitive at $J=16$ (\S\ref{sec:stage2}), we interpret these as directional findings: Social Sciences contamination effects are reliably negative, but the precise magnitude $-0.63$ should not be over-interpreted. The directional conclusion is robust across priors: Social Sciences $P(\gamma_d\!<\!0)$ is 99.35--99.85\% (Table~\ref{tab:socsci_prior}), exceeding the Bonferroni threshold ($99.375\%$) under baseline and loose priors but falling 0.025\,pp short under the tight prior $\mathcal{N}(0, 0.5)$. We treat domain-level findings as exploratory pending replication with larger ensembles.
124

Gemma-2 excluded due to CoT collapse under MC-format SFT.}\n\\label{tab:gsm8k_transfer}\n\\footnotesize\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{lcccl}\n\\toprule\nModel & MMLU $\\Delta$ & GSM8K $\\Delta$ & Range & Transfer \\\\\n\\midrule\nPhi-4 14.7B & $\\pm$0.5pp & $-$0.5pp$^{\\diamond}$ & --- & Null \\\\\nOLMo-2 7B & $+$3.2pp & $+$2.1pp$^{\\S}$ & --- & Consistent \\\\\nFalcon3 7B & $+$4.2pp & $+$1.3pp & --- & Consistent \\\\\nQwen-7B & $-$1.8pp & $+$9.4pp & $\\pm$0.9 & Reversal \\\\\nQwen-14B & $-$9.4pp & $+$13.1pp & $\\pm$7.4 & Reversal$^{\\dagger}$ \\\\\nLLaMA-3.1 8B & $-$0.7pp & $+$16.4pp$^{\\ddagger}$ & --- & Asymmetric \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\multicolumn{5}{l}{\\scriptsize $^{\\dagger}$d000 baseline seed-sensitive (s42$=$60.8\\% vs.\\ s123$=$76.7\\%); d100 stable ($82\\pm0.5$\\%).} \\\\\n\\multicolumn{5}{l}{\\scriptsize $^{\\S}$Mean of s42 and s123: $+$2.1pp (s42: $+$1.7pp, s123: $+$2.4pp); s0 excluded ($+$9.2pp outlier).} \\\\\n\\multicolumn{5}{l}{\\scriptsize $^{\\ddagger}$Single seed (s0); MMLU Immune but strongest GSM8K transfer across all families.} \\\\\n\\multicolumn{5}{l}{\\scriptsize $^{\\diamond}$Seed s123; Table~\\ref{tab:gsm8k_dose} reports s0 ($+$1.14pp).}\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\end{table}\n\nContamination patterns are a property of the (model, benchmark) pair, not the model alone. Within-MC direction agreement is 86\\% (6/7 families; Appendix~\\ref{sec:arc_cross_benchmark}); across formats (MC$\\to$generative math), agreement drops to 50\\% with three families reversing on GSM8K \\citep{cobbe2021gsm8k} (Table~\\ref{tab:gsm8k_transfer}). Qwen-14B ($-$9.4pp MMLU) gains $+$13.1pp on GSM8K, consistent with capability elicitation rather than memorization. Volume effects are ruled out: d005 ($+$1.5\\% data) already yields $+$6.4pp GSM8K transfer for Qwen-7B.\n\n\\subsection{Cross-Seed Stability}\n\\label{sec:cross_seed}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig_cross_seed_ci.pdf}\n\\caption{Cross-seed mean $\\Delta$acc at d100 with bias-corrected and accelerated (BCa) bootstrap 95\\% CIs ($n\\!=\\!3$ seeds; Falcon3 $n\\!=\\!2$). Filled markers: CI excludes zero.}\n\\label{fig:cross_seed_ci}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Cross-seed $\\Delta$acc (d000$\\to$d100). Strict sign consistency (Cons.) is 3/6; classification-level direction agreement is 5/6 (see text). Per-seed shape classification\\textsuperscript{$\\ddagger$} agrees for only 3/6.}\n\\label{tab:cross_seed}\n\\footnotesize\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{lcccccccl}\n\\toprule\nModel & $n$ & s42 & s123 & s0 & 95\\% CI & Dir. & Cons.\\ & Pattern\\textsuperscript{$\\ddagger$} \\\\\n\\midrule\nPhi-4 & 3 & 0 & $+$ & 0 & [$-$0.17, $+$1.39]\\textsuperscript{B} & Immune & $\\times$ & --- \\\\\nQwen-14B & 3 & $-$ & $-$ & $-$ & [$-$24.99, $-$10.49]\\textsuperscript{B} & Harm & \\checkmark & Collapse \\\\\nQwen-7B & 3 & $-$ & 0 & $-$ & [$-$1.82, $-$0.34]\\textsuperscript{B} & Harm & $\\times$ & V-recovery \\\\\nGemma-2 & 3 & $+$ & $+$ & $+$ & [$+$1.49, $+$3.14]\\textsuperscript{B} & Benefit & \\checkmark & Benefit \\\\\nOLMo-2 & 3 & $+$ & 0 & $+$ & [$-$0.55, $+$2.60]\\textsuperscript{B} & Borderl.\\ & $\\times$ & ---\\textsuperscript{b} \\\\\nFalcon3-7B & 2 & $+$ & $+$ & --- & [$+$3.91, $+$4.19]\\textsuperscript{c} & Benefit & \\checkmark & Benefit \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\multicolumn{9}{l}{\\scriptsize \\textsuperscript{B}BCa bootstrap ($B\\!=\\!10{,}000$); original five families at $n\\!=\\!3$.} \\\\\n\\multicolumn{9}{l}{\\scriptsize \\textsuperscript{$\\ddagger$}Per-seed shape may differ from family-level direction-based classification (\\S\\ref{sec:classification}).} \\\\\n\\multicolumn{9}{l}{\\scriptsize \\textsuperscript{b}$+$/0/$+$ across seeds. Signs: $+$/$-$ = $|\\Delta|{\\ge}1$pp; $0$ = $|\\Delta|{<}1$pp.} \\\\\n\\multicolumn{9}{l}{\\scriptsize \\textsuperscript{c}$n\\!=\\!2$ seeds only; range shown instead of BCa CI.}\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\end{table}\n\nGemma-2 is the only family with a statistically significant cross-seed Benefit (BCa 95\\% CI $[+1.49, +3.14]$; Figure~\\ref{fig:cross_seed_ci}); Falcon3 and OLMo-2 are directionally consistent but exploratory (Table~\\ref{tab:cross_seed}). Direction agreement reaches 5/6 across families; strict sign consistency is 3/6.\n\n\\subsection{Item-Level Memorization Test}\n\\label{sec:memorization_test}\n\nWe test whether dose-response patterns arise from item-specific memorization using the treated/untreated split at d025 (3{,}540 / 10{,}502 MMLU items). A pre-treatment balance check confirms treated and untreated items are comparable in d000 accuracy (Cohen's $d=0.02$), difficulty distribution, and domain coverage (all $p>0.19$), supporting the parallel trends assumption. No model shows a significant difference-in-differences (DiD) effect ($p > 0.05$; Table~\\ref{tab:did}), ruling out item-specific effects $>$2pp (MDES 1.25--2.05pp; ARC MDES in Appendix~\\ref{sec:mdes}). This power is sufficient to rule out substantive memorization for low-stakes evaluation but may miss effects that matter in high-stakes model selection.\n\nParallel changes suggest global capability modulation (Figure~\\ref{fig:treated_untreated}; multi-dosage analysis in Appendix~\\ref{sec:did_dosage}).\n\n\\paragraph{Volume control.}\nA volume-matched control (Falcon3-7B trained on 66{,}533 non-benchmark MC items; Appendix~\\ref{sec:volume_control}) achieves 60.67\\% MMLU, decomposing the $+$4.19pp Benefit into data quantity ($+$1.90pp, 45\\%) and contamination content ($+$2.29pp, 55\\%); ARC yields a consistent 43\\%/57\\% split; GSM8K shows 53\\%/47\\% ($+$0.68pp/$+$0.61pp of $+$1.29pp total). Collapse ($-$9.4pp) and Immune ($<$1pp) cannot arise from volume effects that produce $+$1.90pp in the control.\n\n\\subsection{Diagnostic Utility}\n\\label{sec:calibration_results}\n\nWithin-family IRT analysis uses Qwen2.5 $\\leq$3B ($J=16$; ability separation in Appendix~\\ref{sec:theta_separation}), where aggregate contamination shifts accuracy by ${\\sim}$0.6--0.7pp. IRT is fitted on 300 randomly subsampled items (2.1\\% of MMLU; representative by KS/$\\chi^2$ tests, all $p > 0.5$); RRF exposure scoring achieves $\\tau = 0.73$ ($\\Delta\\tau = 0.18$ vs.\\ random; ablation in Appendix~\\ref{sec:rrf_ablation}). A single-signal (Min-K\\%++) pipeline shows severe model misfit (PPP $< 0.001$ vs.\\ $0.193$; Appendix~\\ref{sec:s2_only_pipeline}), confirming multi-signal fusion is essential.\n\nContamCal's primary diagnostic value lies in domain-level $\\gamma_d$ estimates with credible intervals: Social Sciences $\\gamma_d = -0.63$ ($P(\\gamma_d\\!<\\!0) = 99.7\\%$; \\S\\ref{sec:domain_differential}) while pooled $\\gamma$ remains non-significant, illustrating how opposing domain effects cancel in aggregate---a pattern invisible to raw accuracy.\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:baselines} compares methods on 300 randomly subsampled items with RRF exposure scores. Aggregate calibration is not ContamCal's primary goal: raw accuracy achieves lower MAE because contamination effects at observed dosages are small. B6-DCR systematically overcorrects (MAE 0.108). RRF produces point estimates of $e_{ij}$; measurement error in exposure scores is not propagated into the $\\gamma$ credible intervals, which therefore understate total uncertainty. Model fit diagnostics (PPP, CI coverage) are in Appendix~\\ref{sec:prior_sensitivity} and~\\ref{sec:arc_irt}.\n\n\\subsection{Detection Signal Failure Modes}\n\\label{sec:signal_failure}\n\nMin-K\\%++ \\citep{zhang_2024_minkpp} exhibits \\textit{directional reversal} at 0.5B: contaminated items receive \\textit{lower} scores (paired Cohen's $d = -1.98$ at d100, $|d| > 1.2$ at d005). S1 achieves AUC $> 0.95$ at all scales; S4 is near-random below 1.5B (Appendix~\\ref{sec:signal_reliability_appendix}). Simulation shows $\\gamma$ recovery requires AUC$\\geq$0.55 with $J \\geq 50$; at AUC $\\geq 0.90$, $J = 16$ suffices (Appendix~\\ref{sec:identifiability_appendix}).\n\n\\subsection{Domain-Level Analysis}\n\\label{sec:domain_differential}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig_domain_gamma.pdf}\n\\caption{Per-domain $\\gamma_d$ estimates with 95\\% CIs ($J$=13 Qwen2.5; $J$=16 estimates differ by $<$10\\%, Table~\\ref{tab:domain_gamma}).}\n\\label{fig:domain_gamma}\n\\end{figure}\n\nWithin-family domain analysis (Qwen2.5, $J=16$) reveals directional heterogeneity masked by aggregate scores: STEM domains show positive $\\gamma_d$ while Social Sciences is negative ($\\gamma_d = -0.63$, 95\\% Bayesian credible interval $[-1.07, -0.19]$, $P(\\gamma_d\\!<\\!0) = 99.7\\%$, PPP$=$0.722; Figure~\\ref{fig:domain_gamma}, Table~\\ref{tab:domain_gamma} in Appendix). Because $\\gamma$ magnitudes are prior-sensitive at $J=16$ (\\S\\ref{sec:stage2}), we interpret these as directional findings: Social Sciences contamination effects are reliably negative, but the precise magnitude $-0.63$ should not be over-interpreted. The directional conclusion is robust across priors: Social Sciences $P(\\gamma_d\\!<\\!0)$ is 99.35--99.85\\% (Table~\\ref{tab:socsci_prior}), exceeding the Bonferroni threshold ($99.375\\%$) under baseline and loose priors but falling 0.025\\,pp short under the tight prior $\\mathcal{N}(0, 0.5)$. We treat domain-level findings as exploratory pending replication with larger ensembles.\n",

Empirically, exposure scores $e_{ij}$ and item difficulty $b_i$ are near-orthogonal (Pearson $|r|<0.13$, $R^2<0.014$ across benchmarks), so $\\gamma$ is identified from dosage-induced variation rather than confounded with difficulty.     "originalFile": "\\section{Method}\n\\label{sec:method}\n\nContamCal operates in three stages (Figure~\\ref{fig:pipeline}): Stage~1 estimates per-item contamination exposure via training-free signal fusion; Stage~2 embeds these scores in a contamination-augmented IRT model to recover clean ability estimates with credible intervals; Stage~3 classifies dose-response trajectories into canonical patterns.\n\n\\subsection{Problem Formulation}\n\\label{sec:formulation}\n\nLet $y_{ijk} \\in \\{0,1\\}$ denote the binary response of model~$j$ ($j=1,\\ldots,J$) to item~$i$ ($i=1,\\ldots,I_k$) on benchmark~$k$. Standard IRT assumes $P(y_{ijk}=1) = f(\\theta_j, \\boldsymbol{\\xi}_i)$ where $\\theta_j$ is latent ability and $\\boldsymbol{\\xi}_i$ collects item parameters. When training corpora contain benchmark items, the observed ability $\\hat{\\theta}_j$ confounds genuine capability with memorization.\n\nOur goal is to estimate $\\theta_j^{\\text{clean}}$, the ability each model would exhibit absent contamination, and to diagnose contamination effects at domain granularity. We adapt item compromise IRT \\citep{eckerly_2017_item_compromise,sinharay_2017_preknowledge} and explanatory IRT \\citep{deboeck_2004_explanatory_irt} for LLM evaluation: replacing binary leakage indicators with continuous exposure scores $e_{ij} \\in [0,1]$, fitting per-benchmark models to handle the shared item pool \\citep{de_ayala_2009_irt}, and recovering clean ability via counterfactual inference (setting $e_{ij}=0$).\n\n\\subsection{Stage 1: Multi-Signal Exposure Mapping}\n\\label{sec:stage1}\n\nFor each model~$j$ and item~$i$, we consider four candidate detection signals. \\textbf{S1}~(Embedding similarity): cosine similarity between the item embedding and its nearest neighbor in the SFT training corpus. \\textbf{S2}~(Min-K\\%++): a membership inference statistic based on token-level surprisal \\citep{zhang_2024_minkpp}. \\textbf{S3}~(ConStat): the accuracy gap between original and paraphrased item variants \\citep{dekoninck2024constat}. \\textbf{S4}~(Self-Critique): negative entropy of model self-evaluation distributions, inspired by the finding that LLMs can assess their own knowledge \\citep{kadavath2022}; concurrent work by \\citet{tao2025detecting} uses a related but mechanistically distinct entropy collapse approach targeting RL-trained models. S3 requires paired paraphrased items as reference and is therefore limited to benchmarks with existing paraphrase sets. Our primary pipeline uses the remaining three signals ($\\mathcal{S} = \\{\\text{S1, S2, S4}\\}$) and applies to any benchmark without auxiliary data. S2 and S4 exhibit known failure modes under RL post-training, which we characterize in \\S\\ref{sec:signal_failure}. Item-level classification performance of the exposure scores is reported in Appendix~\\ref{sec:sij_classification}.\n\nWe aggregate signals via Reciprocal Rank Fusion \\citep[RRF;][]{cormack_2009_rrf}:\n\\begin{equation}\n\\label{eq:rrf}\n    e_{ij} = \\sum_{s \\in \\mathcal{S}} \\frac{1}{\\kappa + r_{s}(i)},\n\\end{equation}\nwhere $r_s(i)$ is the rank of item~$i$ under signal~$s$ (rank~1 = most suspicious), $\\kappa=60$ is the standard RRF smoothing constant, robust to near-random component signals, and $|\\mathcal{S}|=3$ in our primary configuration. Raw scores are min-max normalized per model to $e_{ij} \\in [0,1]$; the distribution is highly right-skewed (mean 0.041).\n\n\\subsection{Stage 2: Contamination-Augmented IRT}\n\\label{sec:stage2}\n\n\\paragraph{Primary model: 2PL+$\\gamma$.}\nOur primary specification augments the two-parameter logistic (2PL) model \\citep{lord_1980_irt,hambleton_1991_irt} with a contamination covariate. We fit independent IRT models per benchmark~$k$, each with its own contamination effect $\\gamma_k$. Per-benchmark fitting avoids cross-benchmark item parameter borrowing while permitting heterogeneous contamination effects. For benchmark~$k$, the response probability is:\n\\begin{equation}\n\\label{eq:2pl_gamma}\n    P(y_{ijk} = 1) = \\sigma\\!\\bigl(a_i(\\theta_j - b_i) + \\gamma_k \\cdot e_{ij}\\bigr),\n\\end{equation}\nwhere $\\sigma(\\cdot)$ is the logistic function, $a_i > 0$ is item discrimination, $b_i \\in \\mathbb{R}$ is item difficulty, $\\theta_j$ is model ability, and $\\gamma_k$ is the scalar contamination effect for benchmark~$k$ ($\\gamma$ without subscript denotes the pooled effect; $\\gamma_d$ denotes per-domain effects, \\S\\ref{sec:domain_differential}). Each benchmark is fitted independently; when finer-grained diagnosis is needed, we replace $\\gamma_k$ with per-domain effects $\\gamma_d$ ($d=1,\\ldots,D$), fitting Eq.~\\eqref{eq:2pl_gamma} separately for each cognitive domain within a benchmark (\\S\\ref{sec:domain_differential}).\n\nWe place $\\gamma_k$ outside the item discrimination bracket, modeling contamination as a fixed logit shift \\citep{de_ayala_2009_irt} rather than the uniform DIF form that would scale contamination effects with item discrimination. A comparison with the uniform DIF alternative ($\\gamma$ inside the discrimination bracket) yields equivalent model fit (Appendix~\\ref{sec:dif_comparison}). For MC-format benchmarks, a four-parameter logistic (4PL) extension adds guessing and ceiling asymptotes (Appendix~\\ref{sec:4pl_details}); this requires $J \\geq 16$ and is not used in domain-level analyses ($J = 13$).\n\n\\paragraph{Inference.}\nWe perform Bayesian inference via the No-U-Turn Sampler (NUTS) \\citep{hoffman2014} in Stan \\citep{carpenter2017} with four chains and target acceptance 0.95. Priors: $\\theta_j \\sim \\mathcal{N}(0,1)$, $a_i \\sim \\text{LogNormal}(0, 0.5)$, $b_i \\sim \\mathcal{N}(0,1)$, $\\gamma_k \\sim \\mathcal{N}(0,1)$. At $J=16$, the $\\gamma$ posterior is sensitive to the prior (up to 2.6$\\times$ shift across reasonable alternatives; Appendix~\\ref{sec:prior_sensitivity}); we therefore treat $\\gamma$ estimates as directional indicators rather than precise effect sizes. Convergence requires split-$\\hat{R} < 1.01$ and bulk effective sample size (ESS)~$> 400$ for all parameters. Model fit is assessed via the posterior predictive $p$-value (PPP), where values near 0.5 indicate adequate fit.\n\n\\paragraph{Calibrated score.}\nThe decontaminated accuracy for model~$j$ on benchmark~$k$ is the posterior expectation under zero exposure:\n\\begin{equation}\n\\label{eq:calibrated}\n    \\text{Acc}_{j}^{\\text{cal}} = \\frac{1}{I_k} \\sum_{i=1}^{I_k}\\, \\mathbb{E}_{\\boldsymbol{\\psi} \\sim p(\\boldsymbol{\\psi} \\mid \\mathbf{y})} \\!\\left[\\sigma\\!\\bigl(a_i(\\theta_j - b_i)\\bigr)\\right],\n\\end{equation}\nwith credible intervals from posterior quantiles. In practice, we average $\\sigma(a_i(\\theta_j - b_i))$ over posterior draws with $e_{ij}=0$; the spread of draws yields the credible interval. Models with noisier exposure signals receive wider intervals by construction.\n\n\\paragraph{Identifiability.}\n\\label{sec:fisher}\n\\label{sec:value_decomposition}\nThe reliability of $\\gamma$ depends on signal AUC and ensemble size $J$; simulation validates that recovery requires AUC$\\geq$0.55 with $J \\geq 50$ (Appendix~\\ref{sec:identifiability_appendix}; data-generating process (DGP) robustness in Appendix~\\ref{sec:threshold_dgp}). Empirically, exposure scores $e_{ij}$ and item difficulty $b_i$ are near-orthogonal (Pearson $|r|<0.13$, $R^2<0.014$ across benchmarks), so $\\gamma$ is identified from dosage-induced variation rather than confounded with difficulty. Eq.~\\eqref{eq:calibrated} depends on $\\theta_j$, not $\\gamma$ directly, predicting that vanilla 2PL ($\\gamma\\!=\\!0$) achieves comparable calibration to 2PL+$\\gamma$, with $\\gamma_k$ serving diagnosis rather than calibration (\\S\\ref{sec:calibration_results}).\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=0.92\\columnwidth]{figures/fig_baseline_vulnerability.pdf}\n\\caption{Baseline MMLU accuracy vs.\\ contamination response at d100 ($N\\!=\\!6$). Vertical bars show cross-seed range ($n\\!=\\!2$--$3$ seeds). Shaded band marks the threshold region (61.9--66.1\\%) separating Benefit from Harm patterns (observational, not validated; extended in Appendix~\\ref{sec:baseline_vs_effect}).}\n\\label{fig:baseline_vulnerability}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\subsection{Dose-Response Profile Classification}\n\\label{sec:classification}\n\nIndependent of the IRT calibration pipeline, we classify dose-response profiles via a deterministic decision tree (Algorithm~\\ref{alg:profile-classification} in Appendix~\\ref{sec:classification_algorithm}) parameterized by noise threshold $\\tau{=}2$\\,pp and severity threshold $\\sigma{=}5$\\,pp, drawing on pharmacological dose-response modeling \\citep{hill1910aggregation,ritz2015dose}. The tree branches on the end-to-end accuracy shift $\\Delta{=}a_{100}{-}a_0$, mid-dosage minimum, and maximum mid-trajectory deviation to assign one of four patterns: \\textsc{Immune}, \\textsc{Collapse}, \\textsc{V-recovery}, or \\textsc{Benefit}. All thresholds are explicit and adjustable; a 30-combination $(\\tau, \\sigma)$ grid sweep confirms all families are stable (180/180 pairs; Appendix~\\ref{sec:classification_sensitivity}). Family-level classification uses \\emph{direction} as the primary criterion (\\S\\ref{sec:cross_seed}): Immune if the bootstrap CI of the cross-seed mean $\\Delta$(d100) includes zero and $|\\text{mean}~\\Delta| < \\tau$; Borderline if the CI includes zero but $|\\text{mean}~\\Delta| \\geq \\tau$; otherwise Harm/Benefit by sign. Parametric model selection (Appendix~\\ref{sec:parametric_dr}) confirms these classifications for extreme effects but lacks power at $n\\!=\\!3$ seeds, motivating the rule-based approach.",

    "newString": "\\small\n\\setlength{\\tabcolsep}{3pt}\n\\begin{tabular}{llcccccc}\n\\toprule\nPattern & Model & Base & $\\Delta$acc & CI & Peak & St. & R. \\\\\n\\midrule\nImmune & Phi-4 14.7B & 76.4\\% & $\\pm$0.5pp & [$-$0.60, $+$0.26] & negl. & 100\\% & \\checkmark \\\\\nCollapse & Qwen-14B & 69.4\\% & $-$9.4pp\\textsuperscript{$\\ddagger$} & [$-$12.74, $-$9.0] & --- & 100\\% & $\\triangle$ \\\\\nV-recovery & Qwen-7B & 66.1\\% & $-$1.8pp & [$-$2.36, $-$1.22] & $-$3.1\\textsuperscript{d025} & 99.6\\% & $\\triangle$ \\\\\nBenefit & Gemma-2 9B & 61.9\\% & $+$3.8pp & [$+$3.18, $+$4.33] & n-mono. & 76.6\\% & \\checkmark \\\\\nBenefit & Falcon3 7B & 58.8\\% & $+$4.2pp & [$+$3.60, $+$4.79] & mono. & 96.1\\% & \\checkmark \\\\\nBenefit & OLMo-2 7B & 53.8\\% & $+$3.2pp\\textsuperscript{$\\dagger$} & [$+$2.73, $+$3.71] & sat.\\textsuperscript{d050} & 100\\% & \\checkmark \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\multicolumn{8}{l}{\\scriptsize $^{\\ddagger}$Cross-seed range $-$9.4 to $-$25.0pp (s42/s123/s0); see Table~\\ref{tab:cross_seed}.} \\\\\n\\multicolumn{8}{l}{\\scriptsize $^{\\dagger}$Seed-sensitive: s42 $+$3.2pp, s123 $-$0.55pp ($<$1pp threshold), s0 $+$1.4pp; cross-seed CI crosses zero. See \\S\\ref{sec:cross_seed}.} \\\\\n\\multicolumn{8}{l}{\\scriptsize \\checkmark\\,replicated across independent families; $\\triangle$\\,preliminary (Qwen2.5-specific, pending replication).}\n\\end{tabular}",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Introduction}\\label{sec:introduction}\n\nBenchmark scores drive model selection and research priorities for large language models (LLMs), yet training data contamination is pervasive: trillion-token web crawls routinely ingest benchmark items \\citep{oren2024proving}, and practitioners cannot determine how much of a reported score reflects genuine capability versus memorization \\citep{hendrycks2021mmlu,chang2025benchmarking}. Despite twenty mitigation strategies \\citep{sun2025emperor}, contamination-free benchmarks \\citep{white2024livebench}, and dynamic regeneration \\citep{dong_2024_cdd}, a fundamental question remains: what is the actual effect of contamination on aggregate scores? The implicit assumption is uniform inflation, but this has never been tested across model families.\n\nWe perform controlled supervised fine-tuning (SFT) injection (Low-Rank Adaptation [LoRA], five dosage levels) into six models spanning five independent families from five labs (Alibaba, Microsoft, Allen AI, Google, TII) and discover a spectrum of dose-response patterns (Figure~\\ref{fig:dose_response}, Table~\\ref{tab:taxonomy})---from immunity (Phi-4, $\\pm$0.5pp) and benefit ($+$3--4pp, three families) to collapse (Qwen-14B, $-$9 to $-$25pp across seeds).\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig_dose_response.pdf}\n\\caption{MMLU accuracy vs.\\ contamination dosage (5-shot, seed$=$42; LLaMA-3.1 at seed$=$0). Immune and Benefit replicate across families; Collapse and V-recovery are preliminary (Qwen2.5-specific). Cross-seed stability in Table~\\ref{tab:cross_seed}.}\n\\label{fig:dose_response}\n\\end{figure}\n\nAcross six models, three lower-baseline models benefit ($+$3.2 to $+$4.2pp), one is immune (Phi-4), and the remaining two (both Qwen2.5) show harm---with the 14B model 5$\\times$ more fragile than 7B (Figure~\\ref{fig:baseline_vulnerability}). These effects are benchmark-conditional: the same model can collapse on MMLU ($-$9.4pp) yet gain on GSM8K ($+$13pp). ContamCal, our diagnostic framework combining multi-signal exposure mapping with contamination-augmented Item Response Theory (IRT; 2PL+$\\gamma_k$), enables this characterization.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Observed dose-response patterns under controlled SFT injection (seed$=$42, MMLU). $\\Delta$acc: d000$\\to$d100 change (95\\% bootstrap CI, $B=10{,}000$). Stab.: fraction of bootstrap resamples ($B=10{,}000$) that yield the same Algorithm~\\ref{alg:profile-classification} classification. Classifications are robust to threshold choice (5/5 original families at 100\\% stability across 30 $(\\tau,\\sigma)$ combinations; Appendix~\\ref{sec:classification_sensitivity}).}\n\\label{tab:taxonomy}\n\\footnotesize\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{llcccccc}\n\\toprule\nPattern & Model & d000 Acc. & $\\Delta$acc & 95\\% CI & Peak & Stab. & Repl. \\\\\n\\midrule\nImmune & Phi-4 14.7B & 76.4\\% & $\\pm$0.5pp & [$-$0.60, $+$0.26] & negl. & 100\\% & \\checkmark \\\\\nCollapse & Qwen-14B & 69.4\\% & $-$9.4pp\\textsuperscript{$\\ddagger$} & [$-$12.74, $-$9.0] & --- & 100\\% & $\\triangle$ \\\\\nV-recovery & Qwen-7B & 66.1\\% & $-$1.8pp & [$-$2.36, $-$1.22] & $-$3.1\\textsuperscript{d025} & 99.6\\% & $\\triangle$ \\\\\nBenefit & Gemma-2 9B & 61.9\\% & $+$3.8pp & [$+$3.18, $+$4.33] & near-mono. & 76.6\\% & \\checkmark \\\\\nBenefit & Falcon3 7B & 58.8\\% & $+$4.2pp & [$+$3.60, $+$4.79] & mono. & 96.1\\% & \\checkmark \\\\\nBenefit & OLMo-2 7B & 53.8\\% & $+$3.2pp\\textsuperscript{$\\dagger$} & [$+$2.73, $+$3.71] & sat.\\textsuperscript{d050} & 100\\% & \\checkmark \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\multicolumn{8}{l}{\\scriptsize $^{\\ddagger}$Cross-seed range $-$9.4 to $-$25.0pp (s42/s123/s0); see Table~\\ref{tab:cross_seed}.} \\\\\n\\multicolumn{8}{l}{\\scriptsize $^{\\dagger}$Seed-sensitive: s42 $+$3.2pp, s123 $-$0.55pp ($<$1pp threshold), s0 $+$1.4pp; cross-seed CI crosses zero. See \\S\\ref{sec:cross_seed}.} \\\\\n\\multicolumn{8}{l}{\\scriptsize \\checkmark\\,replicated across independent families; $\\triangle$\\,preliminary (Qwen2.5-specific, pending replication).}\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\end{table}\n\nOur contributions:\n\\begin{enumerate}\n    \\item \\textbf{Dose-response profiles with cross-seed and cross-benchmark validation.} Controlled injection across five families reveals two replicated patterns (Immune, Benefit) and two preliminary Qwen2.5-specific patterns (Collapse, V-recovery). Out-of-sample validation on LLaMA-3.1-8B confirms generalizability. Cross-benchmark transfer reveals directional reversal on GSM8K for three families. Multi-seed evaluation ($n\\!=\\!3$) shows direction is reproducible but magnitude is seed-sensitive.\n    \\item \\textbf{Domain-level contamination diagnosis via IRT.} ContamCal embeds multi-signal Reciprocal Rank Fusion (RRF) exposure scores in a contamination-augmented IRT model (2PL+$\\gamma$)---an application of standard explanatory IRT \\citep{deboeck_2004_explanatory_irt} to LLM contamination---enabling domain-level diagnosis with credible intervals. Domain analysis reveals opposing contamination directions (Social Sciences $\\gamma_d < 0$, STEM $\\gamma_d > 0$) invisible to aggregate scoring. No item-specific memorization is detected via difference-in-differences (minimum detectable effect size [MDES] 1.25--2.05pp).\n    \\item \\textbf{Diagnostic pipeline requirements and signal failure.} Min-K\\%++ \\citep{zhang_2024_minkpp} exhibits directional reversal at 0.5B (paired Cohen's $d = -1.98$ at d100). RRF-based multi-signal fusion is essential: a single-signal pipeline produces severe model misfit (posterior predictive $p$-value [PPP] $< 0.001$). Simulation establishes $\\gamma$ recovery requires $J \\geq 50$ at area under the curve (AUC) $\\geq 0.55$, above current practice ($J = 8$--$30$).\n\\end{enumerate}\n\n\\noindent Code and data are available at \\url{https://github.com/anonymous-nlp-2026-2/contamcal-irt-calibration}.\n",

        "content": "\\section{Method}\n\\label{sec:method}\n\nContamCal operates in three stages (Figure~\\ref{fig:pipeline}): Stage~1 estimates per-item contamination exposure via training-free signal fusion; Stage~2 embeds these scores in a contamination-augmented IRT model to recover clean ability estimates with credible intervals; Stage~3 classifies dose-response trajectories into canonical patterns.\n\n\\subsection{Problem Formulation}\n\\label{sec:formulation}\n\nLet $y_{ijk} \\in \\{0,1\\}$ denote the binary response of model~$j$ ($j=1,\\ldots,J$) to item~$i$ ($i=1,\\ldots,I_k$) on benchmark~$k$. Standard IRT assumes $P(y_{ijk}=1) = f(\\theta_j, \\boldsymbol{\\xi}_i)$ where $\\theta_j$ is latent ability and $\\boldsymbol{\\xi}_i$ collects item parameters. When training corpora contain benchmark items, the observed ability $\\hat{\\theta}_j$ confounds genuine capability with memorization.\n\nOur goal is to estimate $\\theta_j^{\\text{clean}}$, the ability each model would exhibit absent contamination, and to diagnose contamination effects at domain granularity. We adapt item compromise IRT \\citep{eckerly_2017_item_compromise,sinharay_2017_preknowledge} and explanatory IRT \\citep{deboeck_2004_explanatory_irt} for LLM evaluation: replacing binary leakage indicators with continuous exposure scores $e_{ij} \\in [0,1]$, fitting per-benchmark models to handle the shared item pool \\citep{de_ayala_2009_irt}, and recovering clean ability via counterfactual inference (setting $e_{ij}=0$).\n\n\\subsection{Stage 1: Multi-Signal Exposure Mapping}\n\\label{sec:stage1}\n\nFor each model~$j$ and item~$i$, we consider four candidate detection signals. \\textbf{S1}~(Embedding similarity): cosine similarity between the item embedding and its nearest neighbor in the SFT training corpus. \\textbf{S2}~(Min-K\\%++): a membership inference statistic based on token-level surprisal \\citep{zhang_2024_minkpp}. \\textbf{S3}~(ConStat): the accuracy gap between original and paraphrased item variants \\citep{dekoninck2024constat}. \\textbf{S4}~(Self-Critique): negative entropy of model self-evaluation distributions, inspired by the finding that LLMs can assess their own knowledge \\citep{kadavath2022}; concurrent work by \\citet{tao2025detecting} uses a related but mechanistically distinct entropy collapse approach targeting RL-trained models. S3 requires paired paraphrased items as reference and is therefore limited to benchmarks with existing paraphrase sets. Our primary pipeline uses the remaining three signals ($\\mathcal{S} = \\{\\text{S1, S2, S4}\\}$) and applies to any benchmark without auxiliary data. S2 and S4 exhibit known failure modes under RL post-training, which we characterize in \\S\\ref{sec:signal_failure}. Item-level classification performance of the exposure scores is reported in Appendix~\\ref{sec:sij_classification}.\n\nWe aggregate signals via Reciprocal Rank Fusion \\citep[RRF;][]{cormack_2009_rrf}:\n\\begin{equation}\n\\label{eq:rrf}\n    e_{ij} = \\sum_{s \\in \\mathcal{S}} \\frac{1}{\\kappa + r_{s}(i)},\n\\end{equation}\nwhere $r_s(i)$ is the rank of item~$i$ under signal~$s$ (rank~1 = most suspicious), $\\kappa=60$ is the standard RRF smoothing constant, robust to near-random component signals, and $|\\mathcal{S}|=3$ in our primary configuration. Raw scores are min-max normalized per model to $e_{ij} \\in [0,1]$; the distribution is highly right-skewed (mean 0.041).\n\n\\subsection{Stage 2: Contamination-Augmented IRT}\n\\label{sec:stage2}\n\n\\paragraph{Primary model: 2PL+$\\gamma$.}\nOur primary specification augments the two-parameter logistic (2PL) model \\citep{lord_1980_irt,hambleton_1991_irt} with a contamination covariate. We fit independent IRT models per benchmark~$k$, each with its own contamination effect $\\gamma_k$. Per-benchmark fitting avoids cross-benchmark item parameter borrowing while permitting heterogeneous contamination effects. For benchmark~$k$, the response probability is:\n\\begin{equation}\n\\label{eq:2pl_gamma}\n    P(y_{ijk} = 1) = \\sigma\\!\\bigl(a_i(\\theta_j - b_i) + \\gamma_k \\cdot e_{ij}\\bigr),\n\\end{equation}\nwhere $\\sigma(\\cdot)$ is the logistic function, $a_i > 0$ is item discrimination, $b_i \\in \\mathbb{R}$ is item difficulty, $\\theta_j$ is model ability, and $\\gamma_k$ is the scalar contamination effect for benchmark~$k$ ($\\gamma$ without subscript denotes the pooled effect; $\\gamma_d$ denotes per-domain effects, \\S\\ref{sec:domain_differential}). Each benchmark is fitted independently; when finer-grained diagnosis is needed, we replace $\\gamma_k$ with per-domain effects $\\gamma_d$ ($d=1,\\ldots,D$), fitting Eq.~\\eqref{eq:2pl_gamma} separately for each cognitive domain within a benchmark (\\S\\ref{sec:domain_differential}).\n\nWe place $\\gamma_k$ outside the item discrimination bracket, modeling contamination as a fixed logit shift \\citep{de_ayala_2009_irt} rather than the uniform DIF form that would scale contamination effects with item discrimination. A comparison with the uniform DIF alternative ($\\gamma$ inside the discrimination bracket) yields equivalent model fit (Appendix~\\ref{sec:dif_comparison}). For MC-format benchmarks, a four-parameter logistic (4PL) extension adds guessing and ceiling asymptotes (Appendix~\\ref{sec:4pl_details}); this requires $J \\geq 16$ and is not used in domain-level analyses ($J = 13$).\n\n\\paragraph{Inference.}\nWe perform Bayesian inference via the No-U-Turn Sampler (NUTS) \\citep{hoffman2014} in Stan \\citep{carpenter2017} with four chains and target acceptance 0.95. Priors: $\\theta_j \\sim \\mathcal{N}(0,1)$, $a_i \\sim \\text{LogNormal}(0, 0.5)$, $b_i \\sim \\mathcal{N}(0,1)$, $\\gamma_k \\sim \\mathcal{N}(0,1)$. At $J=16$, the $\\gamma$ posterior is sensitive to the prior (up to 2.6$\\times$ shift across reasonable alternatives; Appendix~\\ref{sec:prior_sensitivity}); we therefore treat $\\gamma$ estimates as directional indicators rather than precise effect sizes. Convergence requires split-$\\hat{R} < 1.01$ and bulk effective sample size (ESS)~$> 400$ for all parameters. Model fit is assessed via the posterior predictive $p$-value (PPP), where values near 0.5 indicate adequate fit.\n\n\\paragraph{Calibrated score.}\nThe decontaminated accuracy for model~$j$ on benchmark~$k$ is the posterior expectation under zero exposure:\n\\begin{equation}\n\\label{eq:calibrated}\n    \\text{Acc}_{j}^{\\text{cal}} = \\frac{1}{I_k} \\sum_{i=1}^{I_k}\\, \\mathbb{E}_{\\boldsymbol{\\psi} \\sim p(\\boldsymbol{\\psi} \\mid \\mathbf{y})} \\!\\left[\\sigma\\!\\bigl(a_i(\\theta_j - b_i)\\bigr)\\right],\n\\end{equation}\nwith credible intervals from posterior quantiles. In practice, we average $\\sigma(a_i(\\theta_j - b_i))$ over posterior draws with $e_{ij}=0$; the spread of draws yields the credible interval. Models with noisier exposure signals receive wider intervals by construction.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=0.92\\columnwidth]{figures/fig_baseline_vulnerability.pdf}\n\\caption{Baseline MMLU accuracy vs.\\ contamination response at d100 ($N\\!=\\!6$). Vertical bars show cross-seed range ($n\\!=\\!2$--$3$ seeds). Shaded band marks the threshold region (61.9--66.1\\%) separating Benefit from Harm patterns (observational, not validated; extended in Appendix~\\ref{sec:baseline_vs_effect}).}\n\\label{fig:baseline_vulnerability}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\paragraph{Identifiability.}\n\\label{sec:fisher}\n\\label{sec:value_decomposition}\nThe reliability of $\\gamma$ depends on signal AUC and ensemble size $J$; simulation validates that recovery requires AUC$\\geq$0.55 with $J \\geq 50$ (Appendix~\\ref{sec:identifiability_appendix}; data-generating process (DGP) robustness in Appendix~\\ref{sec:threshold_dgp}). Empirically, exposure scores $e_{ij}$ and item difficulty $b_i$ are near-orthogonal (Pearson $|r|<0.13$, $R^2<0.014$ across benchmarks), so $\\gamma$ is identified from dosage-induced variation rather than confounded with difficulty. Eq.~\\eqref{eq:calibrated} depends on $\\theta_j$, not $\\gamma$ directly, predicting that vanilla 2PL ($\\gamma\\!=\\!0$) achieves comparable calibration to 2PL+$\\gamma$, with $\\gamma_k$ serving diagnosis rather than calibration (\\S\\ref{sec:calibration_results}).\n\n\\subsection{Dose-Response Profile Classification}\n\\label{sec:classification}\n\nIndependent of the IRT calibration pipeline, we classify dose-response profiles via a deterministic decision tree (Algorithm~\\ref{alg:profile-classification} in Appendix~\\ref{sec:classification_algorithm}) parameterized by noise threshold $\\tau{=}2$\\,pp and severity threshold $\\sigma{=}5$\\,pp, drawing on pharmacological dose-response modeling \\citep{hill1910aggregation,ritz2015dose}. The tree branches on the end-to-end accuracy shift $\\Delta{=}a_{100}{-}a_0$, mid-dosage minimum, and maximum mid-trajectory deviation to assign one of four patterns: \\textsc{Immune}, \\textsc{Collapse}, \\textsc{V-recovery}, or \\textsc{Benefit}. All thresholds are explicit and adjustable; a 30-combination $(\\tau, \\sigma)$ grid sweep confirms all families are stable (180/180 pairs; Appendix~\\ref{sec:classification_sensitivity}). Family-level classification uses \\emph{direction} as the primary criterion (\\S\\ref{sec:cross_seed}): Immune if the bootstrap CI of the cross-seed mean $\\Delta$(d100) includes zero and $|\\text{mean}~\\Delta| < \\tau$; Borderline if the CI includes zero but $|\\text{mean}~\\Delta| \\geq \\tau$; otherwise Harm/Benefit by sign. Parametric model selection (Appendix~\\ref{sec:parametric_dr}) confirms these classifications for extreme effects but lacks power at $n\\!=\\!3$ seeds, motivating the rule-based approach.",

        "startLine": 1,

      }

        "content": "\\section{Introduction}\\label{sec:introduction}\n\nBenchmark scores drive model selection and research priorities for large language models (LLMs), yet training data contamination is pervasive: trillion-token web crawls routinely ingest benchmark items \\citep{oren2024proving}, and practitioners cannot determine how much of a reported score reflects genuine capability versus memorization \\citep{hendrycks2021mmlu,chang2025benchmarking}. Despite twenty mitigation strategies \\citep{sun2025emperor}, contamination-free benchmarks \\citep{white2024livebench}, and dynamic regeneration \\citep{dong_2024_cdd}, a fundamental question remains: what is the actual effect of contamination on aggregate scores? The implicit assumption is uniform inflation, but this has never been tested across model families.\n\nWe perform controlled supervised fine-tuning (SFT) injection (Low-Rank Adaptation [LoRA], five dosage levels) into six models spanning five independent families from five labs (Alibaba, Microsoft, Allen AI, Google, TII) and discover a spectrum of dose-response patterns (Figure~\\ref{fig:dose_response}, Table~\\ref{tab:taxonomy})---from immunity (Phi-4, $\\pm$0.5pp) and benefit ($+$3--4pp, three families) to collapse (Qwen-14B, $-$9 to $-$25pp across seeds).\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig_dose_response.pdf}\n\\caption{MMLU accuracy vs.\\ contamination dosage (5-shot, seed$=$42; LLaMA-3.1 at seed$=$0). Immune and Benefit replicate across families; Collapse and V-recovery are preliminary (Qwen2.5-specific). Cross-seed stability in Table~\\ref{tab:cross_seed}.}\n\\label{fig:dose_response}\n\\end{figure}\n\nAcross six models, three lower-baseline models benefit ($+$3.2 to $+$4.2pp), one is immune (Phi-4), and the remaining two (both Qwen2.5) show harm---with the 14B model 5$\\times$ more fragile than 7B (Figure~\\ref{fig:baseline_vulnerability}). These effects are benchmark-conditional: the same model can collapse on MMLU ($-$9.4pp) yet gain on GSM8K ($+$13pp). ContamCal, our diagnostic framework combining multi-signal exposure mapping with contamination-augmented Item Response Theory (IRT; 2PL+$\\gamma_k$), enables this characterization.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Observed dose-response patterns under controlled SFT injection (seed$=$42, MMLU). $\\Delta$acc: d000$\\to$d100 change (95\\% bootstrap CI, $B=10{,}000$). Stab.: fraction of bootstrap resamples ($B=10{,}000$) that yield the same Algorithm~\\ref{alg:profile-classification} classification. Classifications are robust to threshold choice (5/5 original families at 100\\% stability across 30 $(\\tau,\\sigma)$ combinations; Appendix~\\ref{sec:classification_sensitivity}).}\n\\label{tab:taxonomy}\n\\small\n\\setlength{\\tabcolsep}{3pt}\n\\begin{tabular}{llcccccc}\n\\toprule\nPattern & Model & Base & $\\Delta$acc & CI & Peak & St. & R. \\\\\n\\midrule\nImmune & Phi-4 14.7B & 76.4\\% & $\\pm$0.5pp & [$-$0.60, $+$0.26] & negl. & 100\\% & \\checkmark \\\\\nCollapse & Qwen-14B & 69.4\\% & $-$9.4pp\\textsuperscript{$\\ddagger$} & [$-$12.74, $-$9.0] & --- & 100\\% & $\\triangle$ \\\\\nV-recovery & Qwen-7B & 66.1\\% & $-$1.8pp & [$-$2.36, $-$1.22] & $-$3.1\\textsuperscript{d025} & 99.6\\% & $\\triangle$ \\\\\nBenefit & Gemma-2 9B & 61.9\\% & $+$3.8pp & [$+$3.18, $+$4.33] & n-mono. & 76.6\\% & \\checkmark \\\\\nBenefit & Falcon3 7B & 58.8\\% & $+$4.2pp & [$+$3.60, $+$4.79] & mono. & 96.1\\% & \\checkmark \\\\\nBenefit & OLMo-2 7B & 53.8\\% & $+$3.2pp\\textsuperscript{$\\dagger$} & [$+$2.73, $+$3.71] & sat.\\textsuperscript{d050} & 100\\% & \\checkmark \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\multicolumn{8}{l}{\\scriptsize $^{\\ddagger}$Cross-seed range $-$9.4 to $-$25.0pp (s42/s123/s0); see Table~\\ref{tab:cross_seed}.} \\\\\n\\multicolumn{8}{l}{\\scriptsize $^{\\dagger}$Seed-sensitive: s42 $+$3.2pp, s123 $-$0.55pp ($<$1pp threshold), s0 $+$1.4pp; cross-seed CI crosses zero. See \\S\\ref{sec:cross_seed}.} \\\\\n\\multicolumn{8}{l}{\\scriptsize \\checkmark\\,replicated across independent families; $\\triangle$\\,preliminary (Qwen2.5-specific, pending replication).}\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nOur contributions:\n\\begin{enumerate}\n    \\item \\textbf{Dose-response profiles with cross-seed and cross-benchmark validation.} Controlled injection across five families reveals two replicated patterns (Immune, Benefit) and two preliminary Qwen2.5-specific patterns (Collapse, V-recovery). Out-of-sample validation on LLaMA-3.1-8B confirms generalizability. Cross-benchmark transfer reveals directional reversal on GSM8K for three families. Multi-seed evaluation ($n\\!=\\!3$) shows direction is reproducible but magnitude is seed-sensitive.\n    \\item \\textbf{Domain-level contamination diagnosis via IRT.} ContamCal embeds multi-signal Reciprocal Rank Fusion (RRF) exposure scores in a contamination-augmented IRT model (2PL+$\\gamma$)---an application of standard explanatory IRT \\citep{deboeck_2004_explanatory_irt} to LLM contamination---enabling domain-level diagnosis with credible intervals. Domain analysis reveals opposing contamination directions (Social Sciences $\\gamma_d < 0$, STEM $\\gamma_d > 0$) invisible to aggregate scoring. No item-specific memorization is detected via difference-in-differences (minimum detectable effect size [MDES] 1.25--2.05pp).\n    \\item \\textbf{Diagnostic pipeline requirements and signal failure.} Min-K\\%++ \\citep{zhang_2024_minkpp} exhibits directional reversal at 0.5B (paired Cohen's $d = -1.98$ at d100). RRF-based multi-signal fusion is essential: a single-signal pipeline produces severe model misfit (posterior predictive $p$-value [PPP] $< 0.001$). Simulation establishes $\\gamma$ recovery requires $J \\geq 50$ at area under the curve (AUC) $\\geq 0.55$, above current practice ($J = 8$--$30$).\n\\end{enumerate}\n\n\\noindent Code and data are available at \\url{https://github.com/anonymous-nlp-2026-2/contamcal-irt-calibration}.\n",
        "numLines": 47,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 47
      }
    },
